**Shah Maruf and Shah Sulaiman** — the author relates that Shah Maruf, a guest at Mangu's house, took
the child Shah Sulaiman in his arms and told the father "یہ لڑکا ہماری امانت ہے۔ اس کی پرورش بخوبی
کرنا" (p. 67, folio 55); and of Shah Sulaiman, that his *wajd* reached the very condition of death
(p. 67, folio 55).

**Shah Chiragh** — the author gives his naming from his father's words on the day of his birth
(p. 68, folio 56).

**Mulla Shah Badakhshi** — the author relates a lifetime without sleep, and hence without ever needing
a ritual bath (pp. 68–69, folios 56–57), and the Shias of Kashmir repenting on coming face to face
with him (p. 69, folio 57). The editor separately reports him versifying his own year of death while
in the throes of it, and offers it as evidence for the date rather than as a marvel (p. 69, folio 57,
حاشیہ 2).

**Nausha Ganj Bakhsh** — the author writes that he was a wali from birth, and gives his father's seven
Hajjs and vision of the Prophet (p. 70, folio 58); his karamat are said to be without end and the
reader is referred to *Tazkira-e Naushahi* (p. 71, folio 59).

**Shah Raza Qadri** — the author writes that whatever he spoke came to pass, and that thousands of
marvels appeared from him involuntarily (p. 73, folio 61).

**Shaikh Muhammad Fazil Batalvi** — the author relates thousands of rupees a day found from the unseen
treasury (p. 76, folio 74).

**Shah Muhammad Ghaus** — the author offers as the one marvel he can vouch for with his own eyes
("چشم دیدہ", p. 77, folio 65) the deaths of Kharak Singh and Nau Nihal Singh after his mazar's inner
enclosure lay in the path of Nau Nihal Singh's demolition (p. 77, folio 75; p. 78, folio 66
[marker: 76]). The same two deaths are told again as retribution in Pir Burhan's entry, below.

**Abdul Rahman Pak and Shaikh Shadi** — narrated by the author as "نقل ہے": a three-fold gift to
Shaikh Shadi, that any sick person he looked at be healed, any dead person he turned to come alive,
and any sinner he looked at become a wali — with the restoration of the dead child on the road
(p. 80, folio 68 [marker: 78]). The author also relates Abdul Rahman Pak's own forty-day burials in a
closed grave and nightly inverted vigils in a well (p. 80, folio 68 [marker: 78]).

**Abdullah Shah Baloch** — the author relates that he told the boy who had ruined his coins with
arsenic and sulphur that if he wanted the operation of alchemy he should come and serve as his father
had, "اور ہم تم کو ایسی کیمیا بخشیں گے کہ یہ کیمیا تمہاری نظر میں خاک نظر آئے گی" (pp. 82–83, folios
70–71 [markers: 80–81]).

**Shaikh Ghulam Husain** — the author says his marvels would need a book of their own and therefore
gives none (p. 83, folio 71 [marker: 81]).

**Qutb al-Din Bakhtiar Kaki** — the author relates that he produced two hot *kak*, that is *kulchas*,
from his armpit at each sama until the whole assembly was fed — "اسی واسطے حضرت کا خطاب کاکی مشہور
ہے" (p. 85, folio 73 [marker: 83]); and his death in the middle of Ahmad-e Jam's ghazal, at the verse
"کشتگان خنجر تسلیم را / ہر زمان از غیب جانے دیگر ست", when "حضرت کا حال برگشتہ ہو گیا اور اسی حالت
میں جان دے دی" (p. 86, folio 74 [marker: 84]).

**Baba Farid al-Din Ganj Shakar**
- the author relates a perpetual fast broken on tree leaves (p. 86, folio 74 [marker: 84]).
- the speaking land at Dipalpur: the land answers "میں اس کی ملکیت تھی جس نے فرید کے پاس مجھ کو
  فروخت کیا اور اب ملکیت خواجہ فرید کی ہوں" and the claimant dies of a broken neck immediately after
  judgement (pp. 88–89, folios 76–77 [markers: 86–87]).
- the flying chairs at Multan: asked how far his austerities had brought him he said "ان کرسیوں کو جس
  پر ہم اور تم بیٹھے ہیں۔ اگر میں کہوں تو ابھی ہوا میں پرواز کر جائیں", and "دونوں کرسیاں زمین سے
  اٹھ کر ہوا کو پرواز کرنے لگیں"; a hand was laid on them with "یہ گفتگو بسبیل تذکرہ تھی نہ بسبیل
  ارشاد۔ تم اسی جگہ پر قائم رہو" (p. 160, folio 148).
- the author relates that he left Delhi for Hansi for the sake of one weeping majzub (p. 236, folio
  224) — told as biography, not as karamat.

**Sabir (Kalyar)** — the author relates that he blew on the big finger of his right hand and "اس پھونک
سے انگلی شمع کے مانند روشن ہو گئی", so that the farman could be read and sealed at night (p. 91,
folio 79 [marker: 89]); that he said "اے مسجد تمام لوگ سجدے کو جاتے ہیں۔ تو بھی سجدہ کر" and the
mosque with roof and walls fell and killed everyone beneath, after which plague and boils came upon
the city (p. 91); and that he annulled another saint's wilayat "آخر سے", from the end, with Baba
Farid refusing to repair it (p. 91).

**Nizam al-Din Awliya** — the author relates that the confectioner's paper covering the halwa turned
out to be the lost jagir farman itself (p. 95, folio 83 [marker: 93]); and, narrated as "نقل ہے", his
celibacy sealed by Baba Farid's instruction that the waist-cord be tied "اس قدر کہ سوائے روز قیامت کے
نہ کھلے۔ اگر کھلے تو حوران بہشت پر کھلے" (pp. 95–96, folios 83–84 [markers: 93–94]). Of him, of Bu Ali
Qalandar and of Shams al-Din Turk alike the author says the marvels fill thousands of books and that
this brief work has no room for them (pp. 93–95, folios 81–83 [markers: 91–93]).

**Khwaja Jamal al-Din Hansvi** — the author records that every farman sent with a khalifa was taken to
him to be sealed (p. 90, folio 78 [marker: 88]).

**Amir Khusrau** — on the authority of the author of *Safinat al-Auliya*: such fire of love was
kindled in his "دل [OCR?] محبت منزل" that his shirt was always burnt away at the place of the heart,
and a new shirt burnt at the heart's place the moment he put it on (p. 97, folio 85 [marker: 95]).
The reading of the phrase is uncertain.

**Chiragh-e Dehli** — the author says the books are full of his marvels and "جن کی گنجائش اس مختصر
میں نہیں ہے", there is no room for them here (p. 98, folio 86 [marker: 96]).

**Shah Kaku** — the author says his karamat are "بہت مشہور" but tells only one: a Sikh said "میں اس کا
امتحان کرتا ہوں", climbed the ber tree over the grave and began to urinate on it, and "اسی وقت پاؤں
پھسل کر نیچے آ پڑا اور ٹانگ ٹوٹ گئی", after which the urs was never again obstructed (p. 100, folio
88 [marker: 98]).

**An unnamed shaikh's practice** — "حق حق حق" shouted into his ear to rouse him from istighraq for the
prayer, reported as his settled practice and not as a marvel, and food for a thousand from the unseen
while travelling (p. 99, folio 87 [marker: 97]).

**Abd al-Karim Lahori** — the author relates that he took a murid to the Hajj and back in an
afternoon: told to shut his eyes and keep a hand on the shaikh's shoulder half a kos out of Lahore,
the murid opened them at Arafat, performed the Hajj, stayed "دوسرے[OCR?] روز" there as well, and
returned to Lahore the same way (p. 104, folio 92). One word's reading is uncertain.

**Shaikh Abu Said** — reported from *Sawati al-Anwar*: to a man who wanted God without the strength
for austerity he said "ہم اس عصا کی تین ضرب سے طالب کو خدا تک پہنچا دیتے ہیں"; the first blow opened
the *alam-e malakut*, the second *jabarut*, the third *alam-e mashhud* (p. 105, folio 93). Separately,
the author relates that, finding no boat at the river after dark between Rampur and Sambhal, he told
the driver to put the *bahli* into the water — "آخر میں بھی اسی گاڑی میں بیٹھا ہوں تو کچھ اندیشہ نہ
کر" — and "گاڑی بان اگرچہ ہندو تھا۔ لیکن توکل بخدا کر کے گاڑی دریا میں ڈال دی"; the cart went over the
water as over land, and on the far bank "گاڑی بان فوراً مسلمان ہو گیا اور مرید ہو کر جامہ درویشی
پہنا" (p. 148, folio 136). Of him the author writes "خوارق و کرامت بہت سے ان کی ذات بابرکات سے سرزد
ہوئے" (p. 148).

**Shaikh Muhammad Sadiq** — reported from *Sawati al-Anwar*: absorbed before a stone idol in the
bazaar he heard it say by God's command "اَنَا المعبود لا تعبد سوائی"; though overpowered in state he
prostrated toward the Kaaba "برعایت شرعِ مقدس"; the idol then said "اینما تولو فثم وجہ اللہ", and the
Shaikh replied that this is true "مگر رعایت شرعِ رسول فرض ہے" (pp. 105–106, folios 93–94).

**Muhammad Arif Lahori** — the author writes in general terms that whatever he uttered came to pass,
and relates a healing in the middle of *wajd*: the qawwals were singing "آن مسیحائے کہ جان در دست
اوست / می دہد جان گر بمیرم چند بار" when a man carried in his dying son, and "حضرت اسی وجد کی حالت
میں اٹھے اور بیمار کے منہ پر ہاتھ پھیرا۔ اسی وقت اچھا ہو گیا" (p. 107, folio 95).

**Shaikh Muhammad Daud** — told from *Sawati al-Anwar*: a murid in a cell containing a ber tree ate its
leaves in a state of "مجہوک[OCR?]" and then claimed forty days without food; the Hazrat said "تو جھوٹ
کہتا ہے" and, on the man's denial, addressed the tree, which "زبان فصیح سے بولا" and said that the
dervish had eaten its leaves for forty days (p. 108, folio 96). One word's reading is uncertain.

**Shah Abu al-Maali (at Thanesar)** — reported from *Thamarat al-Fawad*: at an assembly of mashaikh he
said that death and life lie in the negation and affirmation of "لا الہ الا اللہ"; asked for a test he
said "لا الہ" into the ear of a tethered buffalo and it fell dead, then "الا اللہ" into the other ear
and "فی الفور گاؤ میش جی اٹھی اور چارہ چرنے لگی" (p. 109, folio 97).

**Shaikh Sondha** — reported from *Sawati al-Anwar*: at a village urs he lifted the sheet from a dead
boy's face and said "اے لڑکے اس قادر قیوم کے حکم سے جو ہمیشہ زندہ ہے اور کبھی نہیں مرے گا۔ زندہ ہو
جا", and "لڑکا فی الفور زندہ ہو گیا" (p. 111, folio 99); and his own death in *wajd* to a couplet of
Hafiz (p. 111, folio 99).

**Miran Bhikha** — reported from *Thamarat al-Fawad*: he would not touch the food until his murid's son
was brought — "لڑکا جب تک حاضر نہ ہو گا۔ کھانا ہم پر حلال نہیں ہے" — and when the murid confessed that
the boy had died two *sa'at* before and lay locked in a room, said "وہ مرا نہیں۔ شاید تو نے مغالط
کھایا ہوگا"; the father found him breathing, shook him, and he sat up (pp. 112–113, folios 100–101).
The author also writes that his murids reached the ranks of aqtab and abdal (p. 111, folio 99).

**Fakhr-e Jahan, at Nizam al-Din's khanqah** — from *Manaqib-e Fakhriya*: a young boy in the sama was
found with "سانس کا آنا جانا مسدود اور بدن سرد ہے" after a change of qawwals interrupted the singing;
his father said "یہی ایک میرا فرزند تھا … میں بھی اپنی زندگی نہیں چاہتا"; Fakhr-e Jahan said "شاید
ابھی یہ زندہ ہو" and ordered the qawwals to sing "یک لب لعل تو صد جان می دہد …", after which the youth
stirred and came to his senses (p. 117, folio 105). Of him the author says lakhs of marvels issued
from him (p. 117).

**Alim Allah Jalandhari** — the author says his khalifa's book records many marvels (p. 119, folio
107). The editor summarises his own account of a dream-command that changed his silsila: "لیکن عالم
رویا میں مجھے چشتی سلسلے سے فیض یاب ہونے کا امر ہوا تو میں میران سید بھیکہ کی خدمت میں پہنچا" (p. 347,
folio 335).

**Muhammad Saeed, on the Sharaqpur–Lahore road** — the author relates that his bullock fell and broke
its leg beside the old dome of the Madrasa Aliya, and at midnight "ایک سوار برقع پوش آیا اور اپنے
نیزہ کی نوک کے ساتھ بیل کو اشارہ کیا"; the rider gave his name as "ہمارا نام علی المرتضیٰ ہے اور حق
سے تیری امداد کے لیے مامور ہوئے ہیں" and, asked for inward help, answered "تیرا حصہ شاہ مراد ملتانی کے
پاس ہے۔ وہاں جاکر لے لے" (pp. 121–122, folios 109–110).

**Hafiz Musa** — the author relates that his look in *wajd* left Karim Shah, Muhammad Shah and others
permanently *majzub* (p. 123, folio 111).

**Faiz Bakhsh** — the author relates that he made a fine hole in the bottom of a water pot ("ٹنڈ") and
told the weeping gardener to walk the garden's four sides with it, and "ٹیڈی باہر کے تمام درختوں کو
کھا گئی مگر باغ کے اندر ایک پرند بھی نہ گیا" (p. 126, folio 114); and his death in *wajd* on hearing
Hafiz Qadir Bakhsh sing "منم خاک در کوئے محمد / اسیر حلقہٴ موئے محمد" — "حضرت وجد میں آئے اور لرزہ جسم
پر ظاہر ہوا۔ تمام بدن پر عرق ہو گیا آخر اسی جوش و خروش میں وفات پائی" (p. 126). The author adds that
his murids relate marvels without end, "جس کی گنجائش اس مختصر میں نہیں ہے", "مگر ہزاروں میں سے ایک
تحریر ہوتی ہے" (p. 126).

**The Mujaddid (Ahmad Sirhindi)** — from *Tazkira-e Mujaddidiya*: a man of the Shia Imamiya madhhab
opened the *Maktubat*, saw a page praising Abu Bakr and "فوراً کتاب کو ہاتھ سے پھینک دیا۔ منہ سے بھی
کلمات تبرا کہے"; in sleep he saw the Mujaddid say "اے مردود … تو سزا کے لائق ہے" and drag him before
Ali Murtaza, who struck him; he woke, "آنکھوں سے خون جاری دیکھا", found his face black, repented, and
"مرید ہوتے ہی چہرہ کی سیاہی جاتی رہی" (pp. 129–130, folios 117–118). Of him the author writes "کرامت
و خوارق لاکھوں حضرت کے وجود برکت آمود سے ظاہر ہوئیں۔ جن کا اظہار زبان اور قلم سے نہیں ہو سکتا"
(p. 129, folio 117). The editor, against the text, gives his death year as 1034 AH (p. 130, folio
118).

**Khawand Mahmud** — the author says *Kitab-e Rizwani* records hundreds of his marvels (p. 134, folio
122), and relates that Khan-e Dauran died of a broken neck three days after ordering the rauza pulled
down (p. 134, folio 122).

**Shaikh Muhammad Saeed (of the Mujaddidi line)** — narrated by Mulla Badr al-Din Mujaddidi: the
Hazrat ate the *pan ka bira*, retied the empty palas leaf and threw it to him; he found it empty,
"حاضرین مجلس اس بات پر متبسم ہوئے اور میں نادم ہوا", tucked it into his turban, and at home found it
had become a betel roll and "وہ لذت پائی کہ تمام عمر نہ بھولا" (pp. 134–135, folios 122–123).

**Muhammad Masum** — the author relates that Muhammad Siddiq Peshawari, thrown from his mule and
caught by one foot in the stirrup between Peshawar and Sirhind, "میں زندگی سے ناامید ہوا اور بحالت
ناامیدی حضرت کو یاد کیا", whereupon the Hazrat appeared, seized the mule and freed his foot, and was
gone before he could kiss his feet (p. 136, folio 124).

**Saif al-Din** — the author writes that whoever came before him repented (p. 138, folio 126).

**Nur Muhammad Badayuni** — the author relates that two men "جن کا مذہب شیعہ تھا" came and "امتحاناً
درخواست بیعت کی"; knowing their state "بنور باطنی" he said "اول عقیدہ باطلہ شیعہ سے تائب ہو کر اپنے
قلب کو صاف کرو۔ بعد ازاں بیعت کرنے کا اختیار ہے"; one repented and took bai'at, and "دوسرے کو توفیق
رفیق نہ ہوئی" (p. 141, folio 129). His marvels are called "بے انتہا" (p. 141, folio 129). The editor
separately tells a three-day blockage for a foot put in the wrong order as evidence of his
scrupulousness rather than as a marvel (p. 140, folio 128, حاشیہ 3).

**Abd al-Ahad** — the author relates that he bore an abusive woman's words in silence, then, seeing
that "غیرت الٰہی درپے انتقام ہے", gestured to a servant to slap her; the servant hesitated and "عورت
اسی وقت گر پڑی اور مر گئی"; his rebuke is the point of the story — "اگر تو طمانچے لگانے میں دیر نہ
کرتا تو اس عورت کی جان برباد نہ جاتی … اب منتقم حقیقی نے یہ انتقام لیا اور جان اس کی جاتی رہی۔ خون
اس عورت کا تیری گردن پر ہے" (pp. 141–142, folios 129–130). The woman's grievance is stated plainly:
her son had taken bai'at and become a world-renouncing majzub.

**Shah Ghulam Ali** — from the appendix to *Maqamat-e Mazhari*: a woman asked for a sick man's
recovery and was given bread and a little kebab "بطور تبرک"; at home the kebab had become halwa,
"جانا کہ بیمار جان بر نہیں ہو گا" (p. 146, folio 134). From *Durr al-Maarif*: a murid's daughter died
and he told the wife God would give her a son in her place; she answered "مجھ کو ساٹھ سال کی عمر میں
اب لڑکا ہونا ناممکن ہے" and he said "خداوند تعالیٰ قادر ہے", and she bore a son (p. 146, folio 134);
and a murid told on the road to "گاڑی کو دوڑا کر قافلے سے الگ ہو جاؤ" was spared when "تمام قافلہ کو
رہزنوں نے لوٹ لیا اور وہ ان کی وجہ سے سلامت رہے" (p. 147, folio 135). His marvels are called "بیشمار"
(p. 145, folio 133).

**The Qasuri family's mazar, Kasur** — the author calls the platform that never grows hot a "دائمی
کرامت", a permanent karamat as against the one-off marvels elsewhere in the book (p. 152, folio 140);
of Qasuri himself he says only that his qualities are those of a lover of the Prophet.

**Imam Ali Shah (Gurdaspur)** — the author relates that in Lahore "ایک سنار کی عورت کو سخت آسیب تھا",
a goldsmith's wife severely afflicted by an *asib*, found by day "کبھی کسی چاہ [OCR?] میں پڑی ہوئی
اور کبھی کسی قبر کی مغاک میں چھپی ہوئی"; brought to him, "جب مسکن مبارک سے ایک کوس کا فاصلہ رہ گیا۔
عورت ہوش میں آگئی اور آسیب از خود دفع ہو گیا", and the Hazrat, "بے دریافت حال", said the enemy was
already gone and sent them home (p. 157, folio 145). One word's reading is uncertain.

**Sadiq Ali Shah** — told of him as a living man and as a virtue rather than a marvel: "اس سال میں
حضرت کا جوان لڑکا فوت ہو گیا۔ حضرت نے کمال صبر کیا اور کسی طرح کی شکایت زبان پر نہ لائے" (p. 157,
folio 145).

**A death at sea on the way to the Hajj** — told plainly by the author: "پیک اجل آیا", the courier of
death came (p. 149, folio 137).

**Mir Surkh Bukhari** — reported from *Mazhar-e Jalali*: playing as a boy he came on a janaza outside
the city and, on being told the man would be buried, "جلال میں آگئے اور نعرہ اللہ اکبر مار کے مُردہ کے
منہ سے پردہ اٹھایا اور فرمایا۔ قُم بِاذن اللہ", and "مُردہ فی الفور جی اٹھا اور چالیس برس تک زندہ رہا"
(pp. 162–163, folios 150–151).

**Sadr al-Din Arif** — the author relates that his leftover sherbet, given to a Multan shopkeeper,
revealed the *nasut* and *malakut* to him on the first sip (pp. 163–164, folios 151–152).

**Shaikh Ahmad Mashuq** — reported from *Tarikh-e Firishta*: at his forced wudu "بہت مشکیزے پانی کے
صرف ہو گئے، مگر شیخ کے ہتھول [OCR?] پر پانی رواں نہ ہوا۔ جو پانی ہاتھ پر پڑتا فوراً خشک ہو جاتا"; when
the ulama plunged him into the river "دریا کا پانی ایسا اُبلا جس طرح دیگ دان پر اُبلتی ہے"; and at the
words "ایاک نعبد و ایاک نستعین" in prayer "تمام جسم پھٹ گیا اور بال بال سے خون جاری ہو کر کپڑے تر ہو
گئے", after which he broke off and said "اے بھائیو اب تو میں حائض عورت ہو گیا ہوں اور حیض والی عورت کو
نماز معاف ہے" (p. 164, folio 152). One word's reading is uncertain.

**Baha al-Din Zakariya** — the author says hundreds of karamat appeared from him "جن کی تشریح کتب
اخبار میں درج ہے" (p. 160, folio 148), and relates his standing for his pregnant daughter-in-law as
respect paid to an unborn child (p. 166, folio 154).

**Rukn al-Din / Rukn-e Alam** — the author relates that as a small child he wanted a fawn he had seen
in the jungle and, when he wept, "فی الفور ہرنی مع بچہ جنگل سے نکل کر روبرو آکھڑی ہوئی" (p. 166,
folio 154); and, told as mastery rather than as marvel, three months of seclusion, the order to
Maulana Zahir al-Din for the shroud, and death in the last prostration of his own supererogatory
prayers after leading Maghrib (p. 166, folio 154). Of him the author writes "اسی طرح حضرت کے خوارق
کتابوں میں بہت لکھے ہیں" (p. 166).

**Makhdoom Jahaniyan** — reported from *Mazhar-e Jalali*: when he reached Medina the sharifs demanded
the sanad of his sayyid-hood; he went to the Prophet's roza and said "السلام علیک یا رسول اللہ و ابی
و جدی", and from within a loud voice came "علیک السلام یا ولدی یا قرۃ عینی", after which all accepted
his descent "اور بزرگی آپ کی سب پر ثابت ہو گئی" (p. 169, folio 157). The author says thousands of
marvels issued from him, "جن کی تشریح کتب سیر میں لکھی ہے" (p. 168, folio 156).

**Rajan Qattal** — the author relates that in the king's presence he answered the qazi-zada's trap
with "نہیں ہم نواہون مسلمان کے مقدمہ کے لیے آئے ہیں", then came into *jalal*: "تیری تقریر سے ہم کو
دیانت کی بُو نہیں آتی اور تیری اجل کا وقت اگر نزدیک نہ ہوتا تو ہم تجھ سے تقریر کرتے"; at the words the
man was seized with a pain in the side and thrashed "مرغ نیم بسمل کی طرح", and word came that he was
dead (p. 171, folio 159). Refusing the father's plea, he said the son had already departed and that
God would give a better recompense because "عورت اس کی حاملہ ہے", the child to be "عالم و فاضل و ولی";
the qazi went home and found his wife two months pregnant (p. 171, folio 159). His marvels in books
are said to be countless "مگر لاکھوں میں سے ایک لکھے جاتے ہیں" (p. 170, folio 158).

**Abd al-Jalil** — the author relates that after his ghusl the Divine Name issued three times from his
tongue and all present heard it, so that people thought him still alive and the bier was delayed an
hour (p. 173, folio 161); his brother's book is said to record thousands of marvels (p. 173).

**Shaikh Musa Ahangar** — told from the tazkira, whose title the editor corrects: a beautiful young
woman brought her *takla* to his shop; he gazed at her face for a full hour and she said "تو عجب سفید
ریش بزرگ آدمی ہے کہ بیگانی عورتوں کی طرف شہوت کی نظر سے دیکھتا ہے"; he answered "میں تجھ کو نہیں دیکھتا
بلکہ تیرے مصور کو دیکھتا ہوں", drew the red-hot spindle across his eyes like kohl and prayed "الٰہی
اگر میں نے اس عورت کو بد نظر سے دیکھا ہے تو میری آنکھیں جلا دے۔ ورنہ اس لوہے کے سُرمچو کو اپنی محبت و
عشق کی پارس سے سونا بنا دے"; the iron turned to gold, and the Hindu woman renounced the world that
same day (p. 175, folio 163). The editor holds the author's death year of 925 AH for him to be wrong
(p. 175, folio 163).

**Sayyid Jamal al-Din** — *Jamal-e Irfan* is said to record innumerable marvels of him (p. 177, folio
175 as printed).

**Mauj Darya Bukhari** — the author relates that someone said before him "سید کبھی سنی نہیں ہوتا",
quoting the Punjabi saying "سید سنی نہیں کاٹھ دی کنٹی [OCR?] نہیں", glossed in the text as "سید سنی
نہیں ہے اور لکڑی کی ہنڈیا نہیں ہے"; he called for a wooden pot, made a stove of his two feet, cooked
rice in it, and "خدا کی قدرت سے نہ تو پاؤں جلے اور نہ ہنڈیا لکڑی کی جلی", then said "دیکھو سید بھی
سنی ہے اور ہنڈیا بھی لکڑی کی" (p. 182, folio 180 as printed). The proverb's key word carries `[OCR?]`
and its reading is uncertain.

**Ghore Shah** — the author relates clay horses that granted wishes when he was five years of age, and
that he died "بمجرد فرمانے ان کلمات کے", at the mere speaking of his father's words, because the
boy's wish-granting was disclosing divine secrets (p. 179, folio 177 as printed).

**Hassu Teli** — the author relates a two-stage prescription: poverty cured by an honest scale, and
then wealth cured by renunciation, the second instruction undoing the first result (pp. 180–181,
folios 178–179 as printed).

**Shah Jamal** — the author relates that four storeys of the damdama sank into the ground during a
dance, and that water burned as oil in the lamps at midnight on that same building site (p. 185,
folio 183 as printed).

**Jan Muhammad** — the author relates wealth in one week and revulsion in two, with the *kalima-e
tamjid* a hundred times a day (p. 186, folio 184 as printed). Of Jan Muhammad Thani, two graves that
levelled themselves overnight (p. 189).

**Shah Daula** — the chuhas are reported by the author as a karamat still in operation (p. 186, folio
184 as printed). On his death year the editor states his method: he has preferred the contemporary
*Karamat Nama* over the other reports (p. 184, folio 182 as printed).

**A murshid who came because the Prophet said he would** — the author relates the journey from Delhi
to a cave in Kashmir, months after the promise (p. 178, folio 176 as printed).

**Data Ganj Bakhsh (Ali bin Usman Hujwiri)**
- the author relates that when the mosque was ready he invited the ulama, led the prayer, then "بعد
  نماز سب کو روبقبلہ کھڑا کیا اور کہا کہ دیکھو قبلہ کس طرف ہے فوراً بحکم خلاق اکبر پردے اٹھ گئے اور
  کعبہ سامنے سے نمودار ہوا اور سب نے بچشم ظاہر دیکھ لیا اور اپنے اعتراض سے نادم ہوئے" (pp. 194–195);
  the editor notes that the mihrab-mark of that building is now preserved under a stone.
- the author closes the statement that his wilayat is "آج تک گرم ہے، بلکہ روزافزوں" with
  "اِنَّ اَولِیاءَ اللّٰہ لا یموتون" and his own parenthetical Urdu gloss "(تحقیق اولیائے اللہ کبھی
  نہیں مرتے ہیں ۱۲)" (p. 195).
- the murshid's far-seeing eye, proved by his arrival in Lahore as Husain Zanjani's bier went past:
  the editor reports this as the author's own citation of *Fawaid al-Fawad* and uses it against him
  (p. 198).

**Mian Wada** — the author relates that a young murid's bride, herself a hafiza, refused his company
"جب تک تو قرآن حفظ نہ کرے۔ میری صحبت کے لائق نہیں ہے اور میں نہیں چاہتی کہ ناخواندہ آدمی مجھ سے ہم
صحبت ہو کر قرآن کی بے ادبی … کرے"; the shaikh had him stand at his right at the dawn prayer, and after
the salam "نظر فیض اثر داہنی طرف کے نمازیوں پر پڑی تو سب کے سب قرآن کے حافظ ہو گئے اور بائیں طرف کے
ناظر ہوئے" (p. 188, folio 186 as printed).

**Qalandar Shah, at mauza Sanda** — the author relates that food cooked for five men served a company
that grew until "نوبت پانچ سو آدمی کی پہنچ گئی"; he cast his blessed mantle ("ردائے مبارک") over the
breads, distributed two breads a person and then the curry, "دو سو پچاس کس برتن منقسم ہوا", and when
all had eaten "اسی قدر طعام باقی تھا جس قدر تھا" (p. 190, folio 188 as printed).

**A prince dead on Eid during the siege of Lahore** — "بروز عید ناگاہ مجدود بمرگ مفاجات مر گیا",
told in the entry of the saint who was his tutor (p. 193), and dated by the editor to 10 Zil-Hijja
433 AH / 17 July 1042 CE.

**Baba Bulbul Shah and Raja "Nar Sanjan Deo"** — the author relates that the raja summoned the ulama
of every religion to Kashmir to debate, "مگر بسبب اس کے کہ ہر ایک صاحب ملت اپنے دین کو اچھا کہتا اور
وجوہات بیان کرتا تھا راجہ کی کامل تسلی نہ ہوتی", and settled it in his heart that "کل علی الصباح جس
دین کا آدمی پہلے سب سے میرے روبرو آئے گا۔ اس کا دین اختیار کروں گا"; at dawn Bulbul Shah was praying
at the edge of the Dal, and the raja "اسی وقت … مشرف با اسلام ہوا", most of his nobles following, "اور
نور اسلام کشمیر کی سرزمین پر روشن ہوا" (pp. 202–203). The editor dates it to after 1320 CE.

**Aziz al-Din Makki** — the author relates a six-year reprieve granted to Khusrau Malik under siege in
574 AH, and the fall of his dynasty foretold, fulfilled to the year (p. 201).

**Sakhi Sarwar** — the author relates a spring opened in a waterless wilderness by the blessing of his
step, at the place that became his shrine (p. 197); his martyrdom with his household is recorded
without a miracle, the entry simply giving the year (p. 198).

**Shah Nur al-Din Wali** — per *Tawarikh-e Azami*, greeted in the womb by the rijal al-ghaib (p. 204);
and twenty-six years without bread, in three measured stages, recorded as a spiritual achievement
rather than as a miracle (p. 204). The author also relates that Bibi Lal Ded told his mother
"خداوند تعالیٰ عنقریب تیرے گھر ایک ایسا فرزند پیدا کرے گا جو قطب وقت ہو گا" (p. 205, folio 193).

**Khwaja Ayub** — the author relates that Rumi authorised his own commentator twice: in a student's
dream, "خواجہ ایوب کو اویسی ہماری روحانیت سے پہنچا ہے", and that what he says is Rumi's own intent in
the couplet; and by bibliomancy, the verse "مثنوی را شرح با مشروح دہ" found at the head of the opened
page when he asked leave to write (p. 211, folio 199).

**Fath Shah Sarmast** — the author relates that he sent a servant to the Ravi: "دریا کو جا کر کہہ دو کہ
جدھر سے آیا ہے۔ چلا جا۔ ورنہ قیامت تک خشک کر دیا جائے گا", and "جب یہ پیام دریا کو پہنچا۔ فی الفور
شہر سے دور چلا گیا" (p. 212, folio 200); his title is explained as "بسبب غایت جذب و استغراق" (p. 212).

**Sarmad** — the author gives his passage from "ایک ہندو بچہ پر عاشق ہوا" to "معشوق حقیقی کے عشق" under
the maxim "المجاز قنطرۃ الحقیقت" (p. 209, folio 197), stating the justification in Arabic without
arguing it.

**Shah Gada** — titled for his poverty, a name three of his six khalifas inherit (p. 209).

**Haji Muhammad Saeed** — the author relates that after the plunder of the Punjab a Lahore man told
him the Afghans had carried off his daughter — "اب مجھ کو گھر کے لُٹ جانے کا تو غم کوئی نہیں، مگر لڑکی
کی جدائی نہایت شاق ہے"; told to close his eyes and open them, he found the girl before him, "ایک ہاتھ
میں اس کے چار فلوس تھے اور ایک ہاتھ میں تیل کا برتن تھا", and she said she had been taken to Kabul,
sold to another who made her his slave-girl, and sent that moment to the bazaar for oil, where "یہ
حضرت جو موجود ہیں۔ مجھ کو مل گئے" (pp. 214–215, folios 202–203).

**Abd al-Khaliq Uwaisi** — the author relates that, alone in his hujra reciting darud, he saw "ایک شخص
ماہ طلعت بزرگ صورت" who on the third night gave his name: "ہمارا نام اویس قرنی عاشق رسول ہے۔ ہم تیری
تکمیل و تربیت کے لیے خدا کے حکم سے آئے ہیں", and took his bai'at (p. 219, folio 207); and that on the
fourth day of his unconsciousness "ایک اتفاقاً گانے والا شخص اس راہ سے گذرا", the attendants called
him in, and on hearing the *rag* he came into ecstasy and "اپنے آپ کو کامل و مکمل پایا" (p. 219). From
*Lata'if-e Nafisiya*: he would faint at "Allahu Akbar" and "جب تک گانے والے نہ گاتے ہوش میں نہ آتے"
(p. 220, folio 208).

**Khwaja Qutb al-Din (in the sama)** — the author relates that as a small child, when ecstasy came
upon him, "اللہ اکبر کہہ کر آسمان کو اڑ گیا" and rose so high in a moment that he vanished from
sight, "پھر ان کا نشان روئے زمین پر ظاہر نہ ہوا" (p. 220, folio 208) — a saint with no grave, stated
as such.

**Muhkam al-Din** — the author relates that he dug the ground and gave a beggar a purse of five
hundred rupees; greed made the man dig on, "وہاں سے ایک اور حبہ نہ نکلا", and he found the purse gone
too; the shaikh laughed and said "وہاں کوئی خزانہ مدفون نہ تھا۔ یہ تیرے طمع کی تاثیر تھی کہ وہ تھیلی
بھی جاتی رہی", then dug where he stood and produced the purse again (pp. 220–221, folios 208–209).

**Nur Husain** — the author relates that when thieves took two of his cows and his attendants were
abused, "حضرت غضب میں آئے اور ایک مٹھی گھاس خشک کی ہاتھ میں لے کر اس میں پھونکا تو اس کو آگ لگ اٹھی";
he threw it toward their village saying "ہم نے چوروں کے گاؤں کو جلا دیا ایسا کہ پھر قیامت تک آباد نہ
ہو", and "اسی وقت … تمام و کمال جل گیا" (p. 223, folio 211).

**Maulana Jan Muhammad** — the author relates that after "لاہور میں جب عملداری انگریزوں کی ہوئی تو",
a man named Ghulam Qadir, arrested for theft and opposed by witnesses of his own "بادری [OCR?]", came
to him on the day of the hearing; he "اپنی انگلی سے اس کی پیشانی پر اسم ذات لکھ دیا اور فرمایا کہ جاؤ
تم کو کچھ اندیشہ نہیں", and "جب وہ عدالت میں گیا حاکم نے فی الفور جرم سے رہا کیا" (p. 231, folio 219).
One word's reading is uncertain.

**A case moved from a Dogra court** — the author relates that when Mian Lal Din pleaded that nothing
could be done about a cow-slaughter charge under Hindu rule, "یہ سن کر [OCR?] حضرت متبسم ہوئے اور
فرمایا کہ ہم احکم الحاکمین کے دربار میں اس کی رہائی کے لیے عرض کریں گے۔ چنانچہ وہ ماخوذ اسی روز
مواخذے سے بری ہو گیا" (pp. 233–234, folios 221–222). One word's reading is uncertain.

**Sain Qutb Shah, and the author's own sons** — the author's first-person testimony: "مؤلف کتاب کے دو
فرزند غلام صفدر و اکبر آنکھوں کے عارضہ سے سخت بیمار ہو گئے اور بیماری دو سال تک درازی کھینچ گئی۔ آخر
ایک دوست کے کہنے سے بندہ ایک آثار [OCR?] قند سیاہ اور ایک پاؤ تیل لے کر حاضر ہوا"; Sain Qutb Shah
"دست مبارک ان کے منہ پر پھیر کر کہا کہ اب طبیب کا علاج نہ کرنا", and on the third morning the boys
woke "بالکل تندرست … گویا کبھی ان کو آنکھوں کا عارضہ نہ تھا" (p. 235, folio 223). The measure of black
sugar carries `[OCR?]` and its reading is uncertain.

**The Kabul disputation** — the author relates that when the Shia ulama were at a loss they would say
"آرم ملا رفیع را کہ با سید علی اکبر مباحثہ نماید", "اور ملا رفیع ایک عالم جید ایرانی تھا جو اسی زمانہ
میں مر چکا تھا" — a mockery rather than a marvel (p. 228, folio 216).

**The author's father** — the author records that he missed no prayer in his whole life but three, and
died with his lips moving in dhikr and his fingers telling a rosary that was not there (p. 232, folio
220) — offered as biography.

**Sohan** — the author relates that he was seen "چونے کے انبار کے پاس بیٹھ کر چونہ پھانک رہا تھا" and,
asked why, said "نفس حریص کا پیٹ نہیں بھرتا۔ اس واسطے چاہتا ہوں کہ اس کا پیٹ پتھروں سے بھر دوں"
(pp. 236–237, folios 224–225).

**Allah Din, Jiti Shah and Taj Shah** — hearts read on sight: "اس کے دل کا حال بات بات میں کہہ دیتا"
(p. 237), "فی الفور اس کے مافی الضمیر سے خبر دیتا" (p. 239), "حاضرین کے روبرو ان کے دل کی باتیں بھی
بیان کر دیتا" (p. 244).

**A majzub who vanished with the fort** — the author relates that when Sher Shah ruined the Delhi fort
in 947, "یہ مجذوب گم ہو گیا۔ نہ معلوم کہاں گیا" (p. 237, folio 225); the editor quotes Abd al-Haq to
the same effect, "غائب شد کہ ہیچ نشانی از و پیدا نشد" (p. 237, حاشیہ 3).

**Hasan Lodla** — the author relates that "اکثر بزرگان وقت نے اس کو خواب میں رسول مقبول صلی اللہ علیہ
و آلہ وسلم کے حضور میں دیکھا اور اکثر لوگ جو کہ معظمہ [OCR?] سے آتے۔ وہ کہتے کہ ہم نے اس کو مکہ و
مدینہ میں دیکھا تھا۔ با وجودیکہ وہ کبھی دہلی سے باہر نہ گیا تھا" (p. 238, folio 226) — the place-name
reading is uncertain; and, on the word of Shaikh Haji Muhammad, that Mian Mongar told him in the
Lahore bazaar "تجھ کو لاہور سے کیا غرض ہے۔ ابھی چلا جا۔ چنانچہ وہ اسی وقت دہلی کو اٹھ کر چلا گیا"
(p. 239, folio 227).

**Abu al-Ghaith** — the author relates that at the household urs "یہ آہنی توے گرم کے اوپر دونوں پاؤں
رکھ کر کھڑا ہو گیا اور کچھ اثر جلنے کا پاؤں میں ظاہر نہ ہوا" (p. 238, folio 226).

**Badi Shah** — the author relates "زبان ترجمان اس کی سے جو کلمہ نکل جاتا۔ اسی طرح وقوع میں آتا"
(pp. 239–240) and snow-nights in the jungle "سوائے ایک تہ بند کے" (p. 239, folio 227). The editor gives
the sharper version in Persian from Azam: "بر زبانش حکم شمشیر تیز بران داشت۔ ہرچہ می گفت می شد … بعد
وفات ہم جلال از قبر او ظاہر بود و ہر کہ قسم دروغ می خورد۔ در ساعت بسزای رسید" (p. 239, folio 227,
حاشیہ 2).

**Khwaja Daud (Kashmir)** — the author relates that he said "اچھا آئندہ وبا کے صدمے سے کوئی نہ مرے
گا۔ سب کے عوض میں ہم ہی مرے جاتے ہیں", "زمین پر لیٹ گیا اور جان بحق تسلیم کی", and "اس روز سے کشمیر سے
وبا دفع ہو گئی" (p. 240, folio 228). The editor's Azam has him first send word to Khwaja Habib Allah
Naushahri and Mir Yusuf Qadiri — "عالم خود را فدا سازم ہم ایں گفت و عنقریب رحلت نمود و وبا دفع شد"
(p. 240, حاشیہ 1). The author also records that the ulama of Kashmir repeatedly gave a fatwa for his
execution and the ruler would not act on it.

**Shaikh Mittha** — the author relates that he "وحش و طیور سے ہم کلام ہوتا اور جس شخص پر توجہ کرتا۔ وہ
بھی مجذوب ہو جاتا" (p. 240, folio 228).

**Shah Abdullah** — the author relates that at Naushah's hand, on putting on the worn kurta, "بمجرد
پہننے کے عالم ملکوت اس پر منکشف ہوگیا" (p. 241, folio 229).

**Hafiz Tahir** — the author relates that at Naushah's door he was called by name — "اپنا نام سن کر یہ
متعجب ہوا کہ یہ میرے نام سے کیونکر واقف ہیں" — and restored "ایک نظر فیض اثر سے" (p. 243, folio 231).

**Masum Shah** — the author relates that "اگرچہ اس گھر کی چوبی دہلیز پر بارہ برس تک اس نے آگ جلائی۔ مگر
اس دہلیز کی لکڑی نہ جلی" (p. 243, folio 231); and, on the eyewitness word of the author's friend
Shaikh Wahab al-Din, that he snatched a Hindu widow's cloth "اور وہ کپڑا اس سے چھین کر آگ میں ڈال
دیا۔ چنانچہ جل گیا", then "معصوم شاہ ہنسا اور آگ کی راکھ دور کرکے وہ کپڑا نیچے سے کشیدہ ہوا ہوا نکال
کر عورت کے حوالہ کیا اور کہا کہ مائی جو کام تم سے کئی روز میں ہونا تھا۔ وہ ہم نے ایک روز میں کر دیا"
(pp. 243–244, folios 231–232).

**Taj Shah** — the author relates that on the day Ranjit Singh died he said "نو برس اور یہ سلطنت رہے
گی پھر پنجاب کے مالک فرنگی ہو جائیں گے" (p. 244, folio 232); that he told the dying Maharaja to his
face "مرنا [OCR?] سب کے واسطے ہے … چندن کی لکڑی تیرے جلانے کے لیے لانی چاہیے۔ یہ سن کر وہ نا امید
ہوا۔ جب تاج شاہ قلعے سے نکلا۔ مہاراجہ رنجیت سنگھ نے جان دے دی" (p. 245, folio 233) — one word's reading
is uncertain; and that he promised Nura the carpenter "اب کے تیرے گھر عمر دراز بیٹا ہوگا۔ اس کا نام
لوڈا رکھنا۔ چنانچہ وہ لڑکا پیدا ہوا اور اب تیس برس کی عمر کو پہنچ چکا ہے" (pp. 244–245, folios
232–233).

**Nizam Shah** — the author relates that he came "علی الصباح مسجد محلہ سادھواں میں آیا … کہا کہ آج عید
اضحیٰ کا روز ہے", to the company's astonishment, and "جب پہر دن چڑھا" Raja Hira Singh fled on the
pretext of a hunt and was pursued and killed by the Sikhs (p. 245, folio 233).

**Mastan Shah** — the author relates that "گرما و سرما میں برہنہ بدن پھرا کرتا" and that "جب بھوک
غالب ہوتی تو درختوں کی پتی کھا کر پیٹ بھر لیتا" (p. 246, folio 234); that he read hearts, "گفتگو
گفتگو میں یہ اس کے ما فی الضمیر کا حال کہہ دیتا" (p. 246); and that "ہزاروں خوارق و کرامت اس سے سرزد
ہوئیں۔ جن کا تذکرہ مخلوق کی زبان پر ہے" (p. 246, folio 234).

**Bahle[OCR?] Shah** — the author relates that he "لوگوں کے دل کا حال بر ملا بیان کر دیتا" (p. 247);
the figure's name carries `[OCR?]` and its reading is uncertain.

**The Bibiyan Pak Daman** — the author relates that six women were absorbed into the earth to escape
male eyes, and tells it twice of two different attackers: "چنانچہ سب بیبیاں معہ خدام کے پیوند زمین ہو
گئیں" before the raja of Lahore's army (p. 247, folio 235), and "دعا قبول ہوئی اور زمین نے ان کو اپنے
آپ میں چھپا لیا" before Chingiz Khan's (p. 248, folio 236) — the same karamat serving both
genealogies. On the same page the notes record the judgement, without saying whose it is, that "قیاس
نہیں چاہتا کہ واقعہ کربلا کے وقت یہ عرب سے ہند میں آئی ہوں", reason does not allow that they came
from Arabia at the time of Karbala, while the place remains "نہایت متبرک" (p. 248, folio 236).
Conversion by proximity is told of the same site: "بہت سے لوگ ان کے قدم کی برکت سے مشرف باسلام ہوئے …
جو شخص جاتا۔ وہاں ہی مشرف باسلام ہو کر رہ جاتا" (p. 247, folio 235).

**Shaikh Nizam al-Din Abu al-Mu'ayyad** — attributed to *Akhbar al-Akhyar*: from the pulpit he prayed
"الٰہی میری والدہ کے پہننے کی یہ اوڑھنی ہے۔ جس کی تمام عمر میں نامحرموں کی نظر سے اس کو تو نے محفوظ
رکھا ہے۔ پس اگر وہ عفیفہ پاک دامنہ و محفوظہ تھی تو اس کی پاک دامنی کے طفیل سے اپنی مخلوق کی دعا قبول
کر اور باران رحمت برسا", and "بمجرد اس دعا کے ابر آسمان پر نمودار ہوا" (pp. 249–250, folios 237–238).

**Bibi Zulaikha** — attributed to *Siyar al-Awliya*, *Akhbar al-Akhyar* and *Ma'arij al-Wilayat*: "اس
درخت کے نیچے والدہ کو موجود نہ پایا", and afterwards "حضرت نے جب کیسہ کھول کر دیکھا تو خالی پایا۔ کوئی
ہڈی اس میں موجود نہ تھی۔ معلوم ہوا کہ یہ بھی اسرار الٰہی میں سے تھا" (p. 251, folio 239). Nizam al-Din
is quoted on her: "جب میری والدہ کو کوئی مہم دینی و دنیاوی پیش آتی۔ خداوند تعالیٰ اس کو خواب میں اس
کے انجام سے آگاہ فرما دیتا تھا" (p. 251, folio 239).

**Khusrau Shah** — the author relates that after a prayer at a grave "آئندہ چاند رات کو وہ خسرو شاہ
اپنے مصاحب کے ہاتھ سے مارا گیا" (p. 251, folio 239); the wording is ambiguous as to who kills whom.

**Bibi Qursum Khatun** — the author relates that "جو کچھ زبان مبارک سے فرما دیتی۔ وہی وقوع میں آ جاتا"
(p. 250).

**Bibi Lalla (Lal Devi)** — the author relates that when her pot broke "خدا کی قدرت سے گھڑے کے ٹکڑے
تو زمین پر گر پڑے اور پانی برف کی صورت جم کر اس کے سر پر موجود رہا", and that where she poured the
remainder out "وہاں سے نیا چشمہ جاری ہوگیا" — the place of the spring is not stated (p. 253, folio
241); that she ran "ایک تنور میں کہ آگ اس میں روشن تھی جا چھپی", the people crying "لل دیوی تنور میں
پڑ کر جل گئی", and when called — "لل دیوی یہ کیسا شور تو نے مچا رکھا ہے۔ باہر نکل" — she came out
clothed (p. 253), her cry on seeing Bulbul Shah being "مرد آگیا"; and, on the authority of
*Tawarikh-e Azami*, that "جسم عنصری اس کا مانند روح آسمان کو پرواز کر گیا اور طرفۃ العین میں لوگوں کی
نظر سے غائب ہوگیا" (p. 253, folio 241). The author adds that her marvels are many more, "جن کے اندراج
سے خوف طوالت ہے" (p. 253, folio 241).

**A tree that bowed twice** — the author relates "درخت کو ارشاد کیا کہ پست ہو جا۔ درخت فی الفور پست
ہوگیا اور بی بی نے اس پہ چادر ڈالی تو پھر اونچا ہوگیا", and the husband's scepticism: "جس طرح پر چادر
تو نے درخت پر ڈالی ہے۔ اسی طرح میرے سامنے اتارے تو مجھ کو یقین آتا ہے" (p. 254, folio 242).

**Mai Bhagi** — the author relates that "جو زبان سے کہتی۔ وقوع میں آتا" (p. 255); that two *man* of
wheat fed a khanqah for a year, "گویا کہ وہ دو من غلہ کئی سو من بن گیا", and that Mian Mir rebuked
the display, saying "اظہار کرامت منع ہے اور خدا روزی دہندہ ہے" (p. 255, folio 243); that a man of God
named Zulfiqar asked her for a cup of wine, "اس نے ایک گھونٹ خود پیا اور باقی اس کو دے کر کہا کہ پی
لے۔ بمجرد پینے کے یہ مجذوبہ ہوگئی" (p. 255); and that a post at the Sikh darbar came of her word —
"جا دربار میں دیوانی کا کام کر۔ میرے پیچھے کیوں پڑا ہے۔ پس اسی روز اس کو دیوانی کا کام مل گیا"
(p. 255, folio 243).

**The relics at Makerian fort and Lahore Fort** — the author relates that the fort burned entirely but
the fire reached the upper room holding the relics, below which "اس کے نیچے منزل میں بارود بھرا ہوا
تھا", and "خود بخود منطفی ہوگئی", and that "اس روز سے سدا کور کو ان زیارات کی نسبت نہایت اعتقاد پیدا
ہوا" (p. 258, folio 246). The relic identifications are the book's own attributions: a handprint on
black stone "بعہد خرد سالی شق صدر کے وقت کا" (p. 260, folio 248), a tooth of Uwais Qarani (p. 259,
folio 247), "خاک کربلا معلیٰ خون آلودہ" (p. 259), Quran leaves "ہرن کے چمڑے پر" in the hands of Hasan
and Husain (p. 260), "عَلَم مبارک خاص کربلا کی جنگ کا" (p. 261, folio 249), and a sipara "حضرت کے
دستخطی بخط کوفی" of Ali (p. 259). **The editor rejects the whole provenance**: "تاریخی طور پر ان
زیارات کے پنجاب میں پہنچنے کا کوئی معاصر ثبوت نہیں ہے۔ یہ محض منسوب ہیں" (p. 261, folio 249, حاشیہ
۱). A loss is recorded rather than a miracle: the hairs vanished from their tubes in Hira Singh's
haveli through "بے احتیاطی" (p. 258, folio 246), and the Fort inventory lists the tubes "خالی"
(p. 259).

**Pir Zaki** — from *Tuhfat al-Wasilin*: "آخر شہید ہوا۔ جب سَر اتر چکا۔ جسم بے سَر بھی کفار کے ساتھ
لڑتا رہا", and the two graves follow from it (p. 262, folio 250).

**Sayyid Suf** — reported as locally famous ("اکثر یہ بات مشہور ہے"): "جو شخص محروم ازلی ہوتا ہے۔ اس
کو چلے کے اندر ہی ایسی وحشت دکھلائی دیتی ہے کہ پھر وہ اس مزار پہ نہیں جاتا" (pp. 261–262, folios
249–250).

**Pir Bhola** — the author relates that "جب کوئی لڑکا اس کے پاس آتا یہ بغل سے بزور کرامت شیرینی نکال کر
دیتا", and that "لوگ اکثر اس بات کا امتحان بھی کرتے تھے" (p. 263, folio 251).

**Sultan Bahu** — the author relates of the jungle round his rauza that "اس جنگل کے جس قدر پرند ہیں۔
وہ بھی یا ہُو کا ذکر کرتے ہیں" (p. 265, folio 253).

**Shaikh Fath Shah Amritsari** — attributed to popular report, with no incident given: "کشف و کرامات
اکثر خلقت کے زبان زد ہیں" (p. 266, folio 254).

**Shah Dargahi Qadri** — reported by the author without a source: well water was prescribed and "چنانچہ
اس نے نہلایا تو لڑکا فی الفور اچھا ہو گیا" (p. 268, folio 256).

**Pir az Ghaib** — told as the shrine's known karamat: "کرامت اس کی یہ مشہور ہے کہ جس کے گلے میں ورم
پڑ جائے …", a pebble that cures a swollen throat (p. 270, folio 258). And reported as locally told —
"مشہور اس طرح پر ہے" — that Zeb al-Nisa was building here, "خدا کی قدرت سے اس مقام کی دیوار گر جاتی
تھی", and when the ground was dug "ایک پرانی قبر وہاں سے نکلی" and she had it raised up and built
(p. 270, folio 258).

**Pir Burhan** — the author relates in his own voice that after the demolition "بعد گرانے کے خدا کی
قدرت سے کھڑک سنگھ و نونہال سنگھ باپ بیٹے ایک روز مر گئے اور وہ تجویز موقوف رہی" (p. 271, folio 259).
The same two deaths are claimed for Shah Muhammad Ghaus's shrine, above.

**Shah Rahmatullah Qureshi** — the author gives the miracle and its mundane explanation in the same
sentence: "یہ مزار فرشتوں کا مزار کہلاتا ہے۔ اس سبب سے کہ اس گھر کے معمار مرید بہت تھے۔ جب شاہ رحمۃ
اللہ فوت ہو گئے تو دن کو ان کی کار بسرکار شاہی سے فراغت نہیں ہوتی تھی۔ رات کو انہوں نے جمع ہو کر ایک
رات میں مزار کو تعمیر کر دیا اور مشہور ہوا کہ رات کو فرشتے بنا گئے ہیں" (p. 271, folio 259).

**Shah Farid Naushahi** — the author relates "ناگاہ جاذب حقیقی نے اس کو اپنی طرف کھینچا" (p. 274,
folio 262).

**Shah Gardez Multani** — the author relates that "بعد وفات اظہر کرامت ان کی یہ تھی" that any seeker
who came to the mazar had "حضرت کے دونوں دست مبارک" come out of the grave by a passage left in the
tomb "حسب الوصیت" and shake hands with him; "یہ کرامت مدت مدید تک جاری رہی"; and it ended when "ایک
شخص نالائق نے جو کوئی ایسی بے ادبی کی تو دست مبارک کا باہر آنا موقوف رہا" (p. 276, folio 264).

**The dyers' saint of Lahore** — offered as cause rather than as a specific marvel: "چونکہ ولی کامل
تھے۔ تمام لوگ رنگریزان حضرت کے مرید ہو گئے۔ قبول عظیم پایا" (p. 278, folio 266).

**Shah Hasan** — in an entry that admits the saint's era is unknown, the author writes "مکان نہایت
متبرک اور پر فیض ہے" (p. 279, folio 267).

**Ghulan Shah Sarmast** — the author writes that he "مستانے طریق پر رہا کرتے تھے" (p. 279, folio 267).

**Sayyid Baha' al-Din Baha' al-Haqq and Sayyid Muhammad Muqim, of Hujra Shah Muqim** — attributed to
their descendant Sayyid Inayat Allah Husaini's Arabic preface: "الذین یظھر من یدہ خوارق العادات
لاتحصی" (p. 342, folio 330) and "الذی ظھر من یدہ الکرامات الباھرۃ" (p. 343, folio 331); no specific
miracle is told of either. In the editor's conclusions both founders are "صاحب کرامت", and it is that
reputation that names the place after each in turn — "چونکہ وہ ایک ولی صاحب کرامت تھے ان کی شہرت کے
باعث…" and "اپنی بزرگی اور صاحب کرامات ہونے کے باعث یہ علاقہ حجرہ شاہ مقیم کہلایا" (p. 343, folio
331). The editor also reads a death year out of a chronogram, attributing the hint to the Arabic
preface itself: "''عبدالقادر ثانی'' کے اعداد جمع کئے جائیں تو ان کا سال وفات ۹۷۳ھ بر آمد ہوتا ہے"
(p. 343, folio 331).

**Sayyid Kabir** — "استاد پر روحانیت کا غلبہ تھا ظاہری علوم کی تدریس میں ناغہ ہو جاتا تھا" (p. 347,
folio 335).

**Ahmad Ma'shuq Ilahi** — "اکابر صوفیہ کہ مجالس میں ان کی وارفتگی کو مثال کے طور پر پیش کیا جاتا تھا",
with a "دلنشین" account of him in Nizam al-Din Awliya's majalis, cited to "فوائد الفواد ۴۳۶، ۴۳۹"
(p. 349, folio 337).

### Legends whose figure the notes do not name

- A raiding army converted at a village boundary: "ایک مرتبہ صاحب سنگھ بیدی ان کے گاؤں کے لوٹنے کے
  ارادہ پر آیا لوگ بھاگنے لگے حضرت نے سب کو منع کیا کہ کوئی گاؤں سے نہ نکلے۔ آخر جس قدر لشکر اس کا
  گاؤں میں داخل ہوا۔ سب کے سب مسلمان ہو گئے۔ باقی ماندہ مارے خوف کے گاؤں میں نہ آئے" (p. 222, folio
  210). The notes give the raider's name, Sahib Singh Bedi, but not the saint's.
- Forty cloves that outlast forty days: "بعد چالیس روز کے جب نکلتی ہنوز ان چالیس لونگ سے چند لونگ
  باقی ہوتے" (p. 252, folio 240).
- A warm glance for cold water: a passing faqir "ایک گرم نگاہ سے اس کی طرف دیکھا۔ بمجرد دیکھنے کے
  مستقیم پر بے ہوشی طاری ہوئی۔ فی الفور زمین پر گر پڑی۔ تین دن تک بے ہوش رہا" (p. 244, folio 232).

### Where the book declines to tell the karamat

Recorded here because the refusals are themselves evidence of how the book was made: Shah Husain
(pp. 54–55), Shaikh Ghulam Husain (p. 83), Bu Ali Qalandar, Shams al-Din Turk and Nizam al-Din
(pp. 93–95), Chiragh-e Dehli (p. 98), Fakhr-e Jahan (p. 117), Faiz Bakhsh (p. 126), the Mujaddid
(p. 129), Makhdoom Jahaniyan (p. 168), Rajan Qattal (p. 170), Abd al-Jalil (p. 173), Sayyid Jamal
al-Din (p. 177), Mastan Shah (p. 246) and Bibi Lalla (p. 253). One chunk contains no narrated karamat
at all, while the printer's colophon advertises the book as containing "ان کے خوارق و کرامات کا ذکر"
(p. 283, folio 271).

## Doubts and review points

*Consolidated from 628 "Doubts" findings filed by the chunk workers of chunks 001–032. Ordered by
how much damage the doubt would do if ignored, not by chunk order. Every item carries the PDF page,
the printed folio where the notes give one, and the chunk it came from. Where several chunk workers
raised the same doubt independently, the item says so — that repetition is a severity signal, not a
duplication. Nothing here is resolved: where the evidence points one way the item says so and stops
(CLAUDE.md RULE 2; the decision is Rauf's, RULE 5).*

**Folio caution that applies to every citation below.** The relation `folio = PDF index − 12` holds
from p. 14 to p. 352. The transcription's `[folio N]` markers for **pp. 176–197 are ten too high**,
and chunks 016, 017, 018 and 019 recorded inflated folios for pages in that range. Where an item
cites a folio in that stretch it is written "folio N as printed" and the true value is N − 10. See
Apparatus item A26 for the proof and the amendment this needs.

**Group counts.** Blocking 21 · Identification unresolved 27 · Numerals and dates needing the page
image 102 · Illegible and lost 24 · Internal contradictions 45 · Apparatus problems 27. Total 246
merged items from 628 filed findings.

**The three most urgent, in the compiler's judgement.** (1) **B1, `data-darbar`** — the row's death
year is the one this edition calls "غلط" in terms, and the editor offers no replacement. (2) **B7,
`shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib`** — the row's date and the row's site come from two men
the edition separates as "مختلف شخصیت"; the row may be two saints. (3) **B3,
`darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz`** — three death years are in play and the row's 1041 is none of them, and it
falls inside a governorship the editor dates elsewhere. Close behind, and cheapest to fix: **A26**, the
ten-too-high folio markers for pp. 176–197, which nine chunk workers raised and which mis-points every
folio citation in chunks 016–018.

---

### 1. Blocking — a fact that would enter an archive row wrongly (21 items)

**B1. `data-darbar` — the row's death year is the one this edition calls wrong.** The text gives
**465 AH** three times over in chronograms (p. 195, folio 193 as printed, chunk 018); the editor's
footnote opens "سال وفات میں اختلاف ہے۔ آقائے عبدالحی حبیبی نے کشف المحجوب کے اندرونی…" and breaks
off at "(باقی صفحہ آئندہ)" on that page, continuing into chunk 019, where the year is called
**"آپ کا سال وفات ۴۶۵ھ غلط ہے"** — wrong — and **490 AH [OCR?]** given as the point where the author
is last shown alive. The birth, **400 AH [OCR?]**, is called indeterminate. Both 490s and the 400 are
OCR-flagged. **The editor states no view of his own here, unlike his practice with Shah Daula and
Ayaz.** Actionable: the honest row states the traditional 465 AH, the edition's rejection of it, and
the 490 AH terminus, all three marked unresolved (pp. 195–197, folios 183 as printed / 184–185;
chunks 018, 019 — raised by two workers, the second having read the continuation the first could not).

**B2. `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` — the row's date is the one the editor rejects.** Text and
chronogram give **666 AH** (≈ 1267 CE, the row's current figure); the editor rules for **661 AH**
(≈ 1262–63 CE). The birth is **578 AH** in text and chronogram against **566 AH / 1170 CE** in the
editor's footnote, with a birthplace (**Kot Kahror**) that appears only in the footnote. The editor's
pair gives a life of 95 lunar years and the text's 88; neither is stated as an age anywhere
(pp. 159–161, folios 147–149; chunk 015). Separately, the author's claim of descent from Baha al-Din
Zakariya in seventeen steps carries **no source and no footnote**, from an editor who corrected this
same family's pedigree twice elsewhere — cite it as a family claim (p. 232, folio 220; chunk 022).

**B3. `darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` — three death years are in play and the row's is not one of them.**
Text **450 AH**; editor **449 AH / 1057 CE** on Ibn Athir; Firishta, as the editor reports him, "a few
days after Maudud's death". The row records "**8 August 1041**, as given in the account; calendar era
unstated", which corresponds to neither 449 nor 450 AH and falls **inside** the period the editor
assigns to Majdud's governorship (427–433 AH [OCR?]). **Flag the row rather than amending it from
here.** Also: the shrine's two best-known claims are denied by its own edition — the founding of
Lahore is "later writers' conjecture", and the Mahmud–Ayaz romance is post-Seljuq literary growth,
with the editor offering Ayaz as commander of the cantonment on Latif's own page 45[OCR?] in its place
(pp. 191–193, folios 189–191 as printed; chunk 018).

**B4. `mazar-of-bulleh-shah` — the row's 1757 is supported by nothing in this book, and the row's name
appears nowhere in it.** Author **1180 AH**; editor's finding that he was **still alive in 1181 AH**
(same page, حاشیہ 1); the row's 1757 = 1170–71 AH. The entry heading reads **"سید بلہی [OCR?] شاہ"**,
with the transcriber's flag on the crucial word, and the editor treats the spelling as an open
question resolved only by the seal. The row's "**Abdullah Shah Qadri**" appears nowhere; the book gives
him no personal name beyond Bulha/Bulhe and the attribution "Qadri Shattari Qasuri" (pp. 81–82,
folios 69–70 as printed [markers 79–80]; chunk 007). **The row's date needs a source outside this book
or it needs changing.**

**B5. `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` — the row's date is the one the editor rejects, and two 1141s sit
in one paragraph.** Author's unsourced **۱۱۴۱ھ [OCR?]** against the editor's "alive in 1148 AH, died
approximately after **۱۱۵۰ھ / ۱۷۳۷ء [OCR?]**". **The editor's own footnote uses 1141 AH / 1728 CE for a
different man, Shah Ali Raza Faruqi, in the same paragraph — the two 1141s must not be conflated**
(pp. 75–76, folios 63 and 74 as printed; chunk 006). Address: the row has "Mozang Chungi, Queens
Road"; the appendix has "شاہراہ فاطمہ جناح" (p. 288, folio 276; chunk 027), and the chunk does not say
whether these are the same road — **do not equate them from general knowledge.**

**B6. `ranmal-sharif` — the book places the shrine in a different village from the row.** The editor
states the mazar is "اس وقت ساہنپال شریف ضلع گجرات پنجاب میں", at present at **Sahnpal Sharif, district
Gujrat**, and the *Tazkira-e Naushahi*'s author carries the nisba "ساہنپالوی" (p. 71, folio 59; chunk
006). The row places it at **Ranmal Sharif, Phalia**; **nothing in any chunk names Ranmal or Phalia.**
The figure is beyond doubt — Nausha Ganj Bakhsh, founder of the Naushahia Qadiri order — but the
death year differs by one from the row's 1654: the editor's own publication list gives
"حضرت حاجی محمد نوشہ گنج بخش قادری **ف ۱۶۵۳ء**" with no month, no Hijri year and no source (p. 307,
folio 295; chunk 029). And the name: the entry calls him "**شیخ حاجی محمد قادری**" where the row has
"Syed Muhammad Noushah Ganj Bakhsh"; the chunk gives him **no sayyid descent**, and his pir Shah
Sulaiman's family is claimed as Qureshi by the editor (p. 67, folio 55; chunk 005). "Syed" is
unsupported. **Must be resolved before the row cites this book on location.** (Raised by chunks 006,
023, 028, 029, 031.)

**B7. `shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib` — the row may be two saints, and the edition
says so in terms.** The row's **1042** (≈ 434 AH) fits the *Fawaid al-Fawad* Husain Zanjani whose bier
Data Ganj Bakhsh met; the row's **Chah Miran** fits the **600 AH [OCR?]** Husain Zanjani of entry no.
133. The editor separates them as **"مختلف شخصیت"**, different persons (pp. 196–197, folios 184–185;
chunk 019). **The row needs a decision this book cannot make for it: whether the archive's Zanjani
Sahib is the eleventh-century figure or the thirteenth-century one. Both readings are documented; do
not silently adopt either.**

**B8. `shah-jamal` — thirty-one years and a different lineage.** Book: **14 Rabi al-Thani 1050 AH**
(≈ 1640 CE), a **Sayyid Husaini** in a chain from **Baha al-Din Zakariya Multani**, descendants at
**Sialkot** (p. 185, folio 183 as printed [true 173]; chunk 017). Row: died **1671**, figure "Syed Shah
Jamal Uddin Naqvi Bukhari", silsila Suhrawardi. The silsila and the site (Ichhra, Lahore, the
damdama) agree and the mapping is confident; **the date and the lineage do not, and the book offers no
alternative reading of either.** The book gives this shrine no *urs* date and no custodian.

**B9. `shrine-of-syed-musa-pak` — the author's martyrdom year is rejected by the editor, and the book
asserts a clean succession its own apparatus denies.** Author **1001 AH** against Abdul Haq Dehlvi's
chronogram-derived **998 AH** (p. 53, folio 41, حاشیہ 1). The text says Sayyid Jamal al-Din Musa
succeeded to the *masnad* after his father's death (p. 48, folio 36) while the footnote records a
dispute of "long years" over that same succession (p. 53, folio 41) (chunk 004). The row's
`figure_died` is empty and **this book does not fill it**: the pre-996 AH death of chunk 020 is an
inference from two sentences in different registers — the text's "ان کی وفات کے بعد" and the editor's
dated Hijaz journey — and the text does not say the two events were consecutive (chunk 020).

**B10. `garh-maharaja-shorkot` — the row's 1691 against the book's 1102 AH, and the village is never
named.** The book states only the Hijri year (p. 265, folio 253, حاشیہ ۱) and the month and night in
the colophon; the CE equivalence is arithmetic, not a fact in the chunk. **"Garh Maharaja" does not
occur anywhere**, and "قلعہ شور کوٹ" occurs only as the saint's residence inside the editor's garbled
Persian quotation, not as the site of the rauza. The author's own location statement is a distance:
"جھنگ کے علاقہ میں پندرہ کوس مقام سے جہاں دریائے راوی و چناب ملتے ہیں". The district matches; the site
does not come from this book (p. 265, folio 253; chunk 025).

**B11. `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` — the birth year in the text is not even one of the
candidates the editor weighs, and the whole family history is rejected.** Text **583 AH**, spelled out
"پانسو تیاسی"; the footnote on the same page calls **۵۸۲ [OCR?]** "entirely wrong", discusses
**۵۶۹ [OCR?]** from *Sair al-Auliya*, and settles on **۵۷۱ [OCR?]** from *Fawaid al-Fawad* — all three
footnote figures numerals, all three flagged. **Only the death year, 5 Muharram 664 AH, is affirmed by
both text and editor** (p. 88, folio 76 as printed [marker 86]; chunk 008). The Ghaznavid kinship, the
arrival "from Kabul to Lahore in the time of Shihab al-Din Ghori", the residence at Kasur, the
marriage to Wajih al-Din Khujandi's daughter and the thirty-link chain to Umar are each contradicted
or declared unsupported in three footnotes; the mother's name Qursum Khatun rests on a man "mentioned
by no contemporary tazkira-writer" (pp. 86–87, folios 74–75 as printed; chunk 007). Birthplace spelled
three ways: "بموضع کھوتوال" (p. 88), "قصبہ کتہوال [OCR?]" (p. 86, chunk 007), and the editor's
annotation that the correct name is **Kothewal** (p. 345, folio 333; chunk 032) — where the link to
Baba Farid is **the cited article's title, not the book's sentence**, and the chunk does not say which
saint entry 43 is. Nothing from this part of the entry should enter the row without the footnote
attached. (Raised by chunks 007, 008, 015, 018, 032.)

**B12. `purana-bhalwal` — the row is mapped on the editor's emendation alone.** The text's place name
is **بھیلووال [OCR?]**; only the editor's footnote makes it Bhalwal (p. 67, folio 55, حاشیہ 3). The
name "**Nuri**" and the title "**Sakhi Badshah**" that the row carries appear nowhere. The
identification rests on place + Qadiri + the editor's **1012 AH** against the row's 1604; the author's
own **1065 AH**, which the editor calls wrong, would put him sixty years later (chunk 005). The index
compounds it: "سلیمان بھلوالی | ۵۸" and "سلیمان قادری لاہوری شاہ | ۵۴" are two lines, **neither
carrying both the place and the order** (p. 319, folio 307; chunk 030).

**B13. `darbar-hazrat-khawaja-shah-muhammad-sulaiman-taunsvi-r-a` — the entry's prose year is the
outlier and nothing adjudicates it.** Prose: 9 Safar, a Thursday, "ایک ہزار دوسو سٹھ" (**1260**);
the chronogram immediately below reads "**۱۲۶۷ [OCR?]**"; the editor's footnote in the previous entry
states **1267 AH**. The row carries 1850 CE, which corresponds to 1267 AH, not 1260. Two of the three
readings agree on 1267 and one of those is OCR-flagged (pp. 124, 120; folios 112, 108; chunk 011).
**Record both.** His birthplace is spelled two ways on one page, both flagged — "گڑ گوجھی[OCR?]" and
"گڑ کوچی[OCR?]" — so it cannot be fixed beyond "three kuroh from Taunsa, in the hills" (p. 123, folio
111; chunk 011).

**B14. `madho-lal-hussain` — three death years, a fourth numeral in the wrong place, and the joint
burial never stated.** Shah Husain: text **1008 AH**; *Miftah al-Arifin* **1013 AH**; editor concludes
**999 AH** (p. 55, folio 43 and حاشیہ 2). The chronogram quatrain closing the entry is printed with
"**۹۴۵ھ [OCR?]**" beside it (p. 56, folio 44) — the year the text gives for his **birth**; it must not
be read as a death date. Age at first instruction: seven in the text, ten in 955 AH per
*Haqiqat al-Fuqara* (p. 54, folio 42 and حاشیہ 4) (chunk 004). Madho: birth 983, death 1056, age 73 —
internally consistent, but against the editor's 999 for Shah Husain it leaves Madho as mujawir for 57
years, and everything about Madho rests on *Haqiqat al-Fuqara*, **the book the editor elsewhere calls
careless and uncritical** (chunk 005). **The book names شیخ مادھو as the first of Shah Husain's four
Diwan khalifas and his "معشوق و محبوب" and the editor's article is titled "مادھو لال حسین"
(pp. 55, 57; folios 43, 45), but no chunk states that the two are buried together or that a single
shrine holds them.** Confirm at Madho's own entry, printed p. 52.

**B15. `tahir-bandagi-qadri` — the order is given four ways and the enclosure is unlocated.** Contents
place him at no. 20 inside the **Qadiri** chaman as "شیخ محمد طاہر لاہوری قادری" (p. 15, folio 3,
chunk 001); a footnote makes him a khalifa of the Mujaddid, i.e. **Naqshbandi-Mujaddidi**, dated 1040
AH (p. 56, folio 44, حاشیہ 1, chunk 004); the entry heading reads "**قادری و نقشبندی**" and the entry
calls him "Qutb of his age in the Qadiri silsila" (p. 59, folio 47, chunk 005); the appendix's row 8
reads "شیخ محمد طاہر **قادری نقشبندی**ؒ" — a third written variant — while rows 27 and 61 read
"شیخ طاہر **بندگی**" and "حضرت طاہر **بندگی قادری**" (pp. 288, 290, folios 276, 278, chunk 027).
**The row already carries a recorded discrepancy about his order; this is a fourth reading, not a
resolution.** The enclosure fact — a saint buried inside his chahar-diwari in 1264 AH — comes from a
page that gives him no dates and no location, and names him only "شیخ طاہر لاہوری" (p. 231, folio
219, chunk 022). The row places the shrine in **Miani Sahib**; the chunk's chahar-diwari is
unlocated, and the two are compatible but not shown to be the same. "Bandagi" never appears in the
entry itself. (Raised by chunks 001, 004, 005, 022, 026, 027.)

**B16. `bibi-pak-daman` — "پانچ لڑکیاں" against six names, twice, and it is the book's own
arithmetic.** The heading and the second list both give six — بی بی حاج، تاج، نور، حور، گوہر، شہباز —
while the text counts **five** in both genealogies (pp. 247–248, folios 235–236; chunk 024). The row's
principal figure is "The six Bibis". **Neither author nor editor remarks on the mismatch, and no name
is marked `[OCR?]`, so this is not a transcription artefact.** Two further cautions on the same row:
the 1016 AH burial of Jalal al-Din Haidar dates only the **subsidiary rauza**, and the book does not
say the popular name of that grave is wrong — an entry must not turn the juxtaposition into a
correction the book did not make, and "بدیوار حریم" cannot be resolved between "built into" and
"against" the enclosure wall (chunk 017); and the six names as a set appear in **the editor's table
only** (p. 291, folio 279; chunk 028).

**B17. `shrine-of-mian-mir` — two location columns that do not describe one place, and a burial the
editor's own quotation forbids.** The editor's table gives 1875 "اندر احاطہ خانقاہ حضرت میاں میر"
against 1974 "کراچی ریلوے لائن اور میانی صاحب گاؤں کے درمیان" (p. 291, folio 279; chunk 028). The row
puts Mian Mir at **Alam Ganj (Dharampura)**, not Miani Sahib. **Do not let the 1974 column relocate
the archive's shrine**; the enclosure fact stands on the 1875 column alone. Compounding it: Bibi Jamal
Khatun, whose grave is what the enclosure fact is about, is placed by the author "شہر لاہور میں
**[illegible]** خانقاہ کے اندر" and dated 1000 AH, while the editor quotes Dara Shikoh for 27 Rabi
al-Awwal **1057 AH** and for "**در سیوستان موطن خود اقامت دارند و ہرگز از آں جا بجائے دیگر نرفتہ
اند**" — she never left Sehwan. **If the editor's quotation is right the Lahore burial cannot stand,
and the illegible khanqah name is then doubly unusable** (p. 255, folio 243, and حاشیہ 1; chunk 024).

**B18. `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam` — the day and month do not survive.** 16 Rajab 735 AH in the text
(p. 166, folio 154); the night of Friday, 9 Jumada al-Awwal 735 AH in the Arabic the editor quotes
(p. 165, folio 153). Both are OCR-clean and the year, 735 AH ≈ 1335 CE, matches the row. His birth
year, 647 AH, is **OCR-flagged in the editor's Urdu but spelled out unflagged in the Arabic**
("سنۃ سبع و اربعین و ستمایۃ"), so the Arabic is the better witness (chunk 015).

**B19. Shah Daula (row recorded as 1675; archive id not given in the chunk notes) — the row agrees
with a flagged reading.** Text **1075 AH** with the chronogram "خدا دوست"; editor **۱۰۸۷ھ [OCR?] /
۱۶۷۶ع [OCR?]**; row **1675**. **Both of the editor's figures are OCR-flagged, and his authority,
*Karamat Nama* ورق ۵ [OCR?], is flagged too.** A human must confirm 1087 AH from the printed footnote
before the row's agreement with the editor means anything (p. 184, folio 182 as printed [true 172];
chunk 017). See also L21 — every Shah Daula page reference in these notes is split between pp. 184
and 186 because entry no. 124's text is dislocated.

**B20. `shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari` — do not write that the farman survives.** The chunk gives no
year for the Chittorgarh summons, none for the grant, none for the start of the langar, and no
indication whether the langar was still running when the book was written — the present-tense verbs
belong to the descendants' residence, not to the langar. The farman is "آج بھی … محفوظ", preserved
even today, **but "today" is Sayyid Muhammad Latif's, not the 1974 editor's, and no page of Latif's
*Lahore* is cited. The seal is a nineteenth-century report of a document, relayed in 1974**
(pp. 181–182, folios 179–180 as printed; chunk 017).

**B21. `peer-makki` — the identification is the editor's own and his date is offered only as the more
plausible.** The equation of "شیخ عزیز الدین مکی لاہوری" with "پیر مکی" is the editor's, and it matches
the row, but "**۶۱۲ھ/۱۵۔۱۲۱۶ء**" is endorsed as "زیادہ قرین قیاس" — **more plausible, not
established** — and its CE span is printed in reversed order (p. 350, folio 338; chunk 032). The same
man's death is given as **612 AH** in the author's text (p. 201, folio 189; chunk 019) and as
**ف ۷۹۶ھ [OCR?]** in a footnote quoting the author's *other* book, *Khazinat al-Asfiya* 2/255 (p. 191,
folio 189 as printed; chunk 018) — **a 184-year gap between the author's two books on the same man,
unremarked.** Locality: the row has "Bhati Gate area (near Data Darbar)"; the appendix has
"راوی روڈ پیر مکی سٹریٹ" (p. 290, folio 278; chunk 027), "not shown to conflict; not shown to agree".
